allocation"}, {"path": "knowledge/algorithms/heterogeneous-state-cache.md", "sha256": "268efd94fdf7c091765d06e5542070e6da19ac9e18892ad56dc3632335bdcfa5", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:3422]\n# K-ALG-009：异构状态缓存：层组、恢复位置与联合分配\n\n混合模型保存的是若干更新规则不同的状态族。统一的是逻辑消费位置和计算身份；物理页、块表、检查点与完成事件可以不同。恢复请求必须贯通三个环节：找到全部必需状态共同支持的起点，实际生成目标位置缺失的状态，以及在失败后排空物理使用再归还容量。\n\n## 状态族、输入输出与恢复不变量\n\n用 p 表示已经消费的 token 数，S_g(p) 表示状态族 g 在消费 x[0:p) 后的状态；下一步输入的位置是 p。计算身份 η 包括模型与权重、adapter、输入前缀、位置规则、外部特征、dtype 和量化布局。请求身份、尝试代次和隔离命名空间另用于授权与生命周期管理。跨请求共享可以复用相同计算身份的不可变状态，但每个请求仍须取得自己的持有票据。\n\n输入是 η、目标 p、已知输入范围、各族状态描述、发布与完成票据，以及各池容量。输出是无需模型重算的 DirectPlan、从共同起点 c 推进到 p 的 ReplayPlan，或明确失败原因。DirectPlan 仍可能需要传输和布局转换；只有目标端全部必需状态已经完成、可见并发布，才形成可消费的 RecoveryView。视图包含每族的物理句柄、对象代次、有效逻辑范围、表示和依赖关系。\n\n| 状态族 | 更新方式 | 直接恢复所需内容 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 全注意力 KV | 追加有效位置 | 同一前缀的完整历史 KV |\n| 窗口注意力 KV | 保留最近可见位置，旧槽可能覆盖 | 恢复后第一步所需的完整窗口 |\n| MLA | 追加 latent、位置分量及量化元数据 | 与执行路径相容的压缩表示，不能只核对普通 KV 形状 |\n| 递归状态 | 通常覆盖上一步状态 | 准确消费位置的快照或可执行重放起点 |\n| 局部卷积历史 | 短历史移位或环形覆盖 | 本层投影输入的最近历史，通常不是原始 token |\n| encoder context | 固定源下只读 | 正确源身份、完整范围和表示 |\n\n一个状态族还要记录层集合、更新函数、保留规则、粒度和发布条件。不能因为都叫缓存，就让它们共享相同的回滚与回收规则。\n\n必须分开四个事实：已经分配、已经计算、已经发布、仍然保留。分配页不证明数据完成；增加长度不产生递归状态；传输提交不证明目标可见；目录存在不证明对象还组成完整恢复包。重放的目标位置也只是计划值，不能提前成为已完成长度。\n\n本章使用以下不变量：\n\n- 所有被读状态都属于正确的计算身份、层、逻辑位置和有效范围；物理槽号不代替逻辑位置。\n- 共同直接恢复位置属于所有必需族直接集合的交集；各族最大长度不能代替交集。\n- 重放从全部必需族共同可执行的 c 开始，每个完整模型步骤提交后，各族才共同推进一个位置。\n- 共享历史保持只读；需要写的尾页、窗口槽、递归状态和卷积历史使用私有副本或写时复制。\n- 逻辑引用与物理使用分别结算；所有已提交读写结束前，页与 staging 都不能重用。\n- 多池预留整体成功或不改变容量；失败后按本事务的实际拥有关系归还一次。\n\n这些条件约束正确性。统一池、检查点间隔、逐步提交粒度和淘汰权重则是可比较的设计选择。\n\n## 层组、统一页与实际地址\n\n令 c_g 为一个注意力层、一个 token 的 KV 字节，m_g 为组内层数，P_g 为 token 块长，则组页字节为\n\nM_g＝m_g P_g c_g。\n\nc_g 已包含该层 K 与 V，不能再重复乘二。递归状态按完整快照 B_g 字节计，而非按历史 token 数线性计页；卷积历史、量化 scales 和表示元数据也要进入状态大小。\n\n三种组织都有合法应用：按族独立池使布局和生命周期直接，但可能一池空闲、另一池耗尽；同字节块统一池允许容量调剂，但要求各组物理页字节相同；大块内子分配容纳不同尺寸，却增加对齐、碎片和回收账务。\n\n固定历史 vLLM Hybrid KV Cache Manager 文档把 kv hidden size、block size 与 page size 分开。文档中的 num_layers 是当前组涉及的层数，不是全模型层数。它要求组内 attention 类型相同，使同请求在组内需要相同块数；组间页字节相同，使一个池只有一种分配尺寸。\n\n规则比例可以重复同一组分配结果。不规则层数采用按最小类型层数分组、末组补 padding 的启发式；padding 消耗容量但不产生有效状态。该启发式并非最优性定理，文档也给出应调整组大小的情况。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 BuildGroups\n输入：层类型、每层状态几何、保留规则、支持的块长与对齐约束\n输出：分组、物理布局、padding 账，或统一池不可取的原因\n1 将共享同一实际状态对象的层识别为别名，避免重复分配\n2 按状态类型与保留/更新规则形成候选组\n3 对注意力组计算 m_g * P_g * c_g；对递归组计算完整状态及填充字节\n4 比较各组物理分配字节；不相等时搜索受支持的分组、块长或子分配\n5 对每个候选计有效状态、无效层、页尾、复制粒度与元数据\n6 若没有满足布局和容量约束的候选，改用按族池或其他受支持布局\n7 返回实际组到层、逻辑块到物理片段的映射，不只返回统一页字节\n```\n\n固定文档 case 4 对 Mamba 类不同状态大小的处理是：增大注意力 block size，使 P·c_att≥s_mamba；将每层 Mamba 状态填充到 P·c_att；再使用不规则分组策略。文档指出这可能使块长超过 400，并将另一种含注意力层数的填充方向标为未完成设计。因此统一页只能是带条件的方案：增大页会减少表项，同时可能增大尾浪费、分叉复制和检查点内部碎片。\n\n若使用 LCM，384 与 512 的 gcd 为 128，LCM 为 1536，不是乘积 196608。而且需要对齐的是实际组页字节，不能只对单层每 token 字节取 LCM，再忽略层数与块长。\n\n逻辑块也不必是一段连续物理内存。固定文档对每组 m 层分配 m 个缓冲，各缓冲容纳不同组的一层片段；一个逻辑块映射为 m 个缓冲中的片段。以 m＝2、P＝4、c＝8 B 的独立设定为例，每个层片段为 32 B，一个组块合计 64 B。若某组块表为 [0,3]，则本组层内编号 j 的 token t＝5 位于缓冲 j 的基址＋3×32＋1×8；其中逻辑块号为 1，块内位置为 1。另一组可以使用同一缓冲的其他块号，不能把编号 3 当成每组各自独立的连续大页。\n\n实际 K/V、head、通道与 padding 的片段内偏移须由所选布局继续展开，见[分页 KV](paged-kv-capacity.md)。本章的分组映射不是任意 GPU kernel 的地址 ABI，也不规定 warp 或矩阵指令布局。\n\n## 直接位置集合与目标恢复规划\n\nR_g^direct 表示无需执行模型即可取得可继续状态的位置。它允许搬运稳定副本，但目标消费仍须等待搬运、转换与可见性完成。共同直接集合为\n\nD＝I∩⋂_g R_g^direct，\n\nI 包含输入身份、允许的位置、源对象稳定性及发布资格。若原地递归状态正在推进，不能把它的不同层分别复制后拼成一个快照；应暂停到同一完成位置，或让后续写入进入另一份版本。\n\n窗口宽 W 在这里包含当前 token。下一步 query 位于 p，因此旧状态只需提供\n\n[max(0,p−W＋1),p)\n\n的 KV，当前位置 p 的 K/V 由新一步产生。核宽 K_c 的因果卷积同理需要前 K_c−1 个本层输入。W＝1 或 K_c＝1 时，相应旧历史为空，不能因没有旧槽就拒绝恢复。\n\n为避免 token 下标与消费长度错位，下面所有存储范围均为半开区间 [a,b)：\n\n- 全注意力保存 [0,b) 时，可直接支持消费位置 0…b。\n- 普通窗口保存 [a,b)，W>1 时，正位置 p 要满足上述完整窗口包含于 [a,b)。a＝0 时允许 1…b；a>0 时允许 a＋W−1…b，区间为空则没有正位置。\n- 递归族支持保留检查点集合 C_g；初始化可用时还支持 0。状态 \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:14022]\n整数相等检查当成该保证。\n\n具体轨迹如下：事务 T 的 A、B 两族传输已提交；A 报告失败后，T 停止提交，B 仍在写。此时两个未知完成事件不能减少 T 的在途集合；A 的终结事件只结算 A；B 的取消请求也不结算 B。直到 B 完成或得到可靠取消确认，才能归还目标页和 staging。页面随后交给 T 的下一尝试，旧 A/B 的重复通知只命中旧记录，不改变新尝试。\n\n错误实现若在 A 失败后立即归还页，下一请求可以先写入新内容，随后旧 B 的物理写覆盖它。这个反例必须实际执行释放、重分配和旧写到达，检查新对象内容或“在途操作仍保护原租约”的独立不变量；只计算一个假想 free 向量不构成该负对照。\n\n## 状态包价值与条件化成本\n\n删除一个很小的递归检查点，可能让大量 KV 失去同位置继续执行的价值。设检查点 c 被状态包集合 Q(c) 依赖，删除后包 q 的额外重放步数为 Δ_q(c)，则可用\n\nL(c)＝Σ_{q∈Q(c)} λ_q Δ_q(c)\n\n估计损失；λ_q 将完整模型步折成时间或服务价值。它只能作为策略线索：共享物理对象、未来重用概率、公平性和 deadline 使多个淘汰选择相互影响。若删除后连合法重放起点都不存在，不能只给一个有限额外步数。\n\n以下容量均为结构设定。8 层全 attention、8 层窗口 attention、16 层递归层；attention 每层 Hkv＝8、d＝128、K/V 每元素 2 B，消费长度 4096、窗口保留 256 个位置。单层每 token 为 2×8×128×2＝4096 B。\n\n| 项目 | 算式 | 大小 |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 全 attention | 8×4096×4096 B | 128 MiB |\n| 窗口 attention | 8×256×4096 B | 8 MiB |\n| 递归状态 | 16×4096×16×4 B | 4 MiB |\n| 卷积历史 | 16×3×4096×2 B | 384 KiB |\n| 主项 | 128＋8＋4＋0.375 | 140.375 MiB |\n\n窗口容量采用保留 W 个槽的组织；下一步最少读取 W−1 个旧位置与当前 token，不能把读取下界与物理分配槽数混为一谈。删去 4.375 MiB 的递归和卷积状态，可能让另外 136 MiB 的 attention 状态失去原位置直接恢复资格。\n\n若每 256 token 另存递归及卷积快照，16 份为 70 MiB。这里“另存”假设与当前工作状态不别名；若末端快照与当前不可变状态共享，应按实际唯一对象扣除重复项。设统一页为 512 KiB，每递归层连同卷积历史为 280 KiB，按层独占页时 16 层 padding 为 16×232 KiB＝3.625 MiB。多层打包会改变该账。\n\n六类成本应在同一恢复计划上列出：\n\n- 计算：T_replay(c,p)＝Σ_{t=c}^{p−1} C_step(t)，C_step 是完整模型步骤；目标 1024 从 768 重放需要 256 步，不能只计递归张量更新。检查点构造与格式转换也可能计算密集。\n- 访存：状态读取、候选写入、检查点复制、COW 和块表访问。统一页分配字节不等于 kernel 实际读取字节，更不等于 DRAM 事务量。\n- 通信：对每条共享链路 e，传输下界为 Σ_g B_{g,e}/β_e。独立链路可重叠，但总时间还受逐层依赖关键路径约束；不能只看最快到达的一族。\n- 准备：身份核对、状态资格搜索、重放输入核对、页表与描述符、引用登记、子分配和 block/flat 转换。连续范围覆盖与检查点成员查询均为常数成本时，下降搜索中的状态族资格部分为 O(nF)，n 是候选位置上界，F 是必需族数；逐候选输入扫描的最坏 O(n²) 成本必须另计，不能把 O(nF) 当作完整规划成本。缺页结构另计范围查询成本，资源向量构造、保护与预留另按实际执行次数和处理对象计费。\n- 同步：复制完成、层间依赖、步骤提交、最终发布以及失败排空。超时失败可能很快返回业务结果，但资源继续驻留。\n- 驻留：唯一源对象、目标私有增量、检查点、padding、工作区、元数据和未排空对象的同时存活峰值。预留与其物化页不重复相加，共享页只计一次。\n\n输入扫描的上界来自实际循环。目标 p≤n，候选 c 的区间长为 p−c；即使检查遇到缺项立即短路，总输入位置查询数仍可能达到\n\nQ_input＝Σ_{c=0}^{p−1}(p−c)＝p(p＋1)/2。\n\n若一次查询用常数时间判断该位置的 token 和全部必需特征可重放，则资格搜索加输入扫描最坏为 O(nF＋n²)。这里的常数时间假设针对已经可查询的输入记录，不包括读取外部特征文件、校验大对象或等待数据到达。若每位置还需检查 A 个独立特征项且每项为常数成本，输入扫描可达 O(n²(1＋A))；一般应累计实际检查项的成本。基线扫描只需常数额外游标空间，不创建输入可用性前缀表，原有输入和状态目录的驻留另计。\n\n一个能达到二次查询量的失败轨迹是：目标 p＝n，全 KV 只保留 [0,n−1)，没有窗口、卷积或递归族限制，初始化可用；重放输入仅有位置 0…n−2，缺少最后的 x_{n−1}。目标 n 不能直接恢复，n−1…0 都是合法直接起点。每个候选都从自己的 c 开始，直到缺失的 n−1 才失败，因此依次查询 1、2、…、n 次，最后仍返回缺少输入。\n\n| n | 输入位置成员查询数 n(n＋1)/2 |\n|---:|---:|\n| 64 | 2080 |\n| 128 | 8256 |\n| 256 | 32896 |\n\n固定 F 后，该轨迹仍为 Θ(n²)。失败规划没有执行模型重放，也没有进入保护与资源预留，二次工作已经发生在准备阶段。若补齐最后一个输入，最高合法起点 n−1 只需一次输入查询即可返回重放计划；因此只检查成功结果或只测完整输入，会遗漏这条成本路径。本章保留上述基线及其真实成本，不声称已经实现输入区间预处理。\n\n检查点间隔 Δ 增大通常减少快照字节和写流量，但增加重放距离；窗口淘汰可能让部分递归检查点根本不再是共同起点，所以平均重放不能无条件按 Δ/2 估计。恢复性能应量到第一个正确继续步骤，生成场景还要包含取得合法 logits 的成本。\n\n## 条件变化、反例与区分实验\n\n窗口扩大后，应重算所需历史与共同位置；旧窗口缺失时不能只扩一个长度字段。页长增大后，表项可能减少，但短请求尾浪费、共享分叉复制和统一页 padding 可能使选择反转。递归状态远大于注意力块时，可以放弃统一池，比较按族池、子分配或分层驻留。模型或表示改变时，旧状态只有经过明确的等价转换才可保留；仅总字节相同不构成复用资格。\n\n复用变稀或前缀很短时，快照驻留和写带宽可能超过避免的重算；昂贵长前缀、较短重用距离则提高包级保留价值。完成延迟增大时，隔离待回收容量上升，应降低准入或调整预留，不能通过提前回收维持吞吐。长前缀且输入记录不完整时，准备搜索本身可能成为显著工作，应先核对输入查询量，再决定是否值得改用带明确构建、更新和失效成本的输入索引。\n\n固定 Hybrid 文档给出 full attention 加一种高效 attention 的两类型交集思路，并说明多类型、无 full attention 的组合及 Mamba 前缀支持存在当时的实现限制。其布局示例又包含多个同类型层组，不能把“两种类型”机械解释为全模型只能有两个层组。本章的一般集合与事务算法是独立推导，不宣称该历史实现已经支持任意混合模型。\n\n反例有不同的可观察后果。只取全 KV 命中长度会把 1024 错报为共同直接位置；复制但跳过重放会留下 S(768)；逐层提前发布会拼出不同位置的状态；源端原地推进期间复制递归层会形成撕裂快照；未知事件递减计数会伪造排空；提前释放后旧 DMA 到达会污染新请求；漏计输入重复扫描则会在规划结果正确时仍低估准备工作。不能把这些都归为一种异步错误。\n\n区分实验应固定其余条件并给出可反驳预测：\n\n- 固定准入请求、有效长度与计算图，分别改变 padding、块表取址和准入预算。若固定请求集后差距消失，优先支持容量与排队解释；若仍存在，再独立比较消费者和准备/复制路径。\n- 固定输入，改变检查点间隔，记录共同直接位置、完整模型重放步数、快照写字节和峰值。若重放缩短而总时间变长，应转查快照写带宽、驻留或同步，不能继续只按步数预测。\n- 对共享检查点负载比较单页 LRU 与包级淘汰，记录删除后丢失的共同起点及回退距离。若包级策略没有减少回退，却增加碎片，应修订其价值估计或放弃该策略。\n- 对同一多层输入比较从零执行与各切点恢复，逐层核对 KV、卷积、递归和后续输出。参考按完整序列逐层计算，缓存路径按 token 推进；破坏高层输入、漏一段历史或跳过重放应产生差异。\n- 延迟一个真实写者，注入未知、重复、字段错误和旧代次完成；正确轨迹不提前释放。负对照真正提前释放并重用页面，再让旧写到达，由独立物理租约/内容不变量发现污染。若只改事件到达顺序就失败，先查结算与生命周期，而非数值容差。\n- 固定状态族数量与查询表示，使用上述缺少末端输入的轨迹，扩大 n 并直接统计输入成员查询；预测为 n(n＋1)/2，而非随 n 线性增长。随后仅补齐末端输入，预测最高合法起点一次查询即成功。若准备时间改变但计数不变，再检查单次查询、身份校验或资源操作的成本；操作计数本身不等于时间测量。\n\n交付的 Python 检查定义的是确定性 CPU 模型：连续范围元数据、四层标量 attention/卷积递归模型、合成页池与物理事件账，以及基线规划的输入成员查询计数。它包含独立可见集合参考、逐层稠密参考和真实执行错误转移的负对照；不是引擎源码或真实设备测试。成本检查用原有 plan 路径计数，对照三角数和固定预期值，并拒绝错误的线性查询预算。其执行状态见作者记录，不以正文说明替代实际运行结果。真实 GPU 数值、访存事务、跨 stream/NIC 可见性、分布式故障和性能仍需对应实现与设备证据。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-VLLM-HYBRID：vLLM：Hybrid KV Cache Manager](../sources/S-VLLM-HYBRID.md)：固定历史设计文档区分 KV hidden bytes、token block size 和物理 "87d1433d4f64d939ff8f137d1c1d4092cfdf2b3d7ebdd011a0f344cc66564714", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-ENGINE-003：执行视图决策：压紧、固定槽与结果归属\n\n动态请求集合需要转成设备能执行的张量，但逻辑身份不应随物理行号变化。执行器的核心问题是保持请求、query 位置、缓存写入与输出行之间的可逆对应，并管理这些表示的生存期。\n\n## 从逻辑请求到本轮张量\n\n设请求 i 本轮有 qᵢ 个 query，压紧偏移 p₀=0、pᵢ₊₁=pᵢ+qᵢ；请求 i 的第 k 个 query 位于 pᵢ+k，其绝对位置由对应请求的有效起点与 k 决定。仅需要末位置 logits 的普通生成中，候选行是 pᵢ₊₁−1；需要 prompt logprobs、分支或投机验证时，所需行集合另行表达，不能复用这个简化规则。\n\n设 q=[2,5,3]，则 p=[0,2,7,10]，三个末位置行是 [1,6,9]。若请求被重排，必须同时重排身份映射、位置与结果路由；shape 正确不能发现把第 6 行交给另一请求的错误。\n\n```pseudocode\n更新本轮有效请求集合，保存每个物理槽对应的逻辑身份和代次\n按所选表示生成 query 位置、输入 token 与缓存写入位置\n建立输出行到请求/位置/分支的映射，再提交设备计算\n按该轮映射解释 logits 和采样结果，校验返回代次\n最后消费者完成后才覆盖本轮输入、metadata 或辅助缓冲\n```\n\n## 压紧与固定容量的交换\n\n压紧减少无效行，却付出收集、前缀和、重排或 metadata 更新；固定槽与图桶方便稳定地址和复用，但可能计算填充行并增加多桶驻留。[已核对的执行器实例](../sources/S-VLLM-RUNNER.md) 维护持久批状态，再压紧本轮 query；它说明这两个选择可以组合，并不要求独立引擎复制同一类结构。\n\n容量 16 的桶承载 10 个有效行时，有 6/16=37.5% 的槽位未承载有效行；这只是槽位比例，不直接等于浪费的运行时间。是否跳过无效行、kernel 效率和图重放成本决定实际收益。若每步都重新整理大块历史，压紧的准备成本也可能超过避免的填充工作。\n\n## 用映射与完成证据区分解释\n\n同一请求单独执行、混合批执行、行重排和取消后复用槽位，应得到相同语义结果。记录每轮身份、绝对位置、缓存槽和 logits 路由，可将错配与模型数值误差分开。延迟设备完成再安排下一轮，能检查固定地址是否被过早覆盖。比较准备、设备、采样与可见输出的完整时间线，避免把省下提交工作误当作数学计算变少。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-VLLM-RUNNER：vLLM — GPUModelRunner source "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:4000]\n# K-MODEL-004：SSM 与混合模型：递归状态、扫描与恢复\n\n递归模型把历史压缩进有限维状态，避免每一步读取完整历史，却把前缀复用和回滚变成了准确位置的状态恢复问题。当前状态能支持继续计算，不代表它能还原过去任意位置；服务端保存的检查点、卷积历史、混合层 KV 和输出边界必须共同满足下一步的依赖。\n\n## 递推、选择性与充分状态\n\n令 p 表示已经消费的有效输入数，S(p) 表示恰好消费 x[0:p) 后的模型状态，下一步消费位置为 p。对一个实值、对角结构的选择性 SSM，单请求递推可以写成\n\nh_t[d,n]＝ā_t[d,n]h_{t−1}[d,n]＋b̄_t[d,n]v_t[d]，\nr_t[d]＝Σ_n C_t[n]h_t[d,n]＋D_skip[d]v_t[d]。\n\nD 是 SSM 内部通道数，N 是每通道的状态维度；h 的形状为 D×N，v_t 是实际送入 SSM 的表示。B_t、C_t 在本式中各有 N 个分量并在通道上广播；存在按通道或分组产生系数的模型时，必须改用其实际轴。batch 轴是多个独立请求，不能参与时间递推的合成。\n\n选择性体现为 Δ_t、B_t、C_t 依赖本层输入。固定这些系数后，更新对旧 h 仍是仿射的；整个网络作为输入序列的函数则一般不是线性的。这正是既能按内容选择历史、又能使用仿射扫描的原因。若系数还依赖尚未求出的旧 h，不能直接沿用这里的扫描算法。\n\n以零阶保持离散化为例，令 z＝Δ_t[d]A[d,n]，则\n\nā_t[d,n]＝exp(z)，\nb̄_t[d,n]＝Δ_t[d]B_t[n]φ(z)，\nφ(z)＝expm1(z)/z，φ(0)＝1。\n\nΔ 是离散步长，z 必须无量纲；A、Δ 的单位或参数化应相容。接近零时使用稳定计算或极限，直接计算 exp(z)−1 会出现消减误差。这里展开的是一种明确的离散化，不声称所有 Mamba 制品都采用完全相同的 B̄ 计算。实际 checkpoint 若使用其他离散化或直接离散参数，必须同时替换逐步与扫描路径。\n\n递归状态大小 D×N 不随历史长度增长，短因果卷积还需要最近 K_c−1 个卷积前投影输入。完整混合模型的 S(p) 则包含所有递归层、卷积历史、全注意力 KV、窗口状态，以及解释它们所需的逻辑位置和表示身份。原始 token 通常不能直接替代高层卷积历史，因为后者来自本层前向输入。\n\nS(p) 的充分性针对“给定下一输入后继续计算”。立即生成下一 token 还需要末行 logits，或一个已采样但尚未消费的 pending token，以及随机、grammar、停止和外部输出状态。不能把已生成 token 数、已消费位置和已对外提交数量合成一个长度。\n\n## 从块计算图到完整单步\n\n为明确数据来源，下面选一个 pre-norm、双输入分支、深度因果卷积、ZOH SSM 和输出门控组成的说明性块。它可独立实现，但不是未核验 checkpoint 的接口规范。输入宽度为 H，内部宽度为 D，Δ 的投影秩为 R。卷积权重 w[d,k] 的 k＝0 表示当前项；SiLU(a)＝a·sigmoid(a)，Norm 的类型、轴和参数由固定模型定义。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 ConsumeRecurrentBlock\n输入：x[H]、旧 h[D,N]、旧 ring[K_c−1,D]、逻辑位置 p\n      固定参数 W_u[D,H]、W_z[D,H]、卷积权重和偏置\n      W_B[N,D]、W_C[N,D]、W_q[R,D]、W_delta[D,R]、b_delta[D]\n      A[D,N]、D_skip[D]、W_out[H,D]、Norm 参数\n输出：块输出 x_next[H]、候选 h_new、候选 ring_new\n1 要求 D,N,R,K_c≥1；核对状态维度、请求、计算身份和消费位置\n2 a←Norm(x)\n3 u←W_u a；z_gate←W_z a；window←ring 连接 u\n4 对 d：\n    conv[d]←conv_bias[d]+Σ_{k=0}^{K_c−1} w[d,k]·window[K_c−1−k,d]\n    v[d]←SiLU(conv[d])\n5 B←W_B v；C←W_C v；q←W_q v\n6 Δ←softplus(b_delta+W_delta q)，逐通道计算\n7 对 d,n：\n    z←Δ[d]·A[d,n]\n    alpha[d,n]←exp(z)\n    beta[d,n]←Δ[d]·B[n]·φ(z)·v[d]\n    h_new[d,n]←alpha[d,n]·h[d,n]+beta[d,n]\n8 对 d：\n    r[d]←Σ_n C[n]·h_new[d,n]+D_skip[d]·v[d]\n    g[d]←r[d]·SiLU(z_gate[d])\n9 x_next←x+W_out g\n10 若 K_c=1：ring_new←空；否则取 window 的末 K_c−1 项\n11 返回块输出与候选状态；完整模型全部必要层及输出完成后才提交 S(p+1)\n```\n\n初始化可以采用零 h 和零历史，也可以采用模型规定的非零初态。初始补零只适用于不存在的历史；恢复一个非零位置时不能再次补零。实际块若有额外归一化、不同门控位置、bias 或其他残差，必须按原图调整，不能凭相同算子名交换顺序。\n\n多个独立文档打包时，若任务定义文档间不相互依赖，应在边界重置所有相关递归和卷积状态，并采用相应位置规则。只修改 attention mask 不会隔断递归状态。batch 行重排同样必须把 h、ring、位置和输出归属按同一请求映射重排；只移动 h 会把另一请求的局部历史接进当前前向。\n\n## 仿射扫描与分块推进\n\n把一个状态坐标的单步更新写成 T_t(h)＝a_t h＋b_t。定义有顺序的合成\n\nCombine(earlier,later)＝(a_later a_earlier，a_later b_earlier＋b_later)。\n\n它表示先执行 earlier，再执行 later，等于 T_later∘T_earlier。展开三步，两种括号都得到 a₃a₂a₁h＋a₃a₂b₁＋a₃b₂＋b₃，因此在实数上满足结合律；单位元是 (1,0)。对角状态逐坐标应用即可，不能把矩阵乘法顺序任意交换。\n\n下面采用补齐到二次幂的树扫描。先向上保存区间摘要，再向下传播每个区间之前的摘要，最后求各位置的包含式前缀。每个结点有唯一写者；并行实现的每个树层之间必须建立完成与可见性依赖。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 AffinePrefixScan\n输入：a[L,J]、b[L,J]、h0[J]；J 可表示展开后的 D×N\n输出：states[L,J]、末状态 h_end[J]\n1 要求 J≥1；a、b 行数均为 L，且每行宽度恰为 J\n2 若 L=0：返回空 states 和 h0 的副本\n3 Q←不小于 L 的最小二次幂\n4 对每个独立坐标 j：\n    建立 tree[1:2Q)、prefix[1:2Q)，全部初始化为单位元\n    对 t=0…L−1：tree[Q+t]←(a[t,j],b[t,j])\n    对 k=Q−1…1，按树层从叶到根：\n       tree[k]←Combine(tree[2k],tree[2k+1])\n    prefix[1]←单位元\n    按树层从根到叶，对每个内部结点 k：\n       prefix[2k]←prefix[k]\n       prefix[2k+1]←Combine(prefix[k],tree[2k])\n    对 t=0…L−1：\n       inclusive←Combine(prefix[Q+t],tree[Q+t])\n       states[t,j]←inclusive.a·h0[j]+inclusive.b\n5 h_end←states[L−1] 的副本；返回 states、h_end\n```\n\n这份算法每坐标的工作量为 O(Q)，并行深度 O(log Q)，Q<2L；L＝1 时没有内部结点，叶的前缀就是单位元。系数宽度与 h0 不同属于输入合同错误，应明确拒绝，不能依靠索引异常或静默广播。多维单步也必须为每个状态坐标提供相应系数。\n\n分块 prefill 的系数来自本层已经正确形成的整块输入。网络层仍按计算图顺序执行：高层输入依赖低层输出，不能同时把不同层串进一个仿射扫描。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 ChunkedRecurrentBlock\n输入：本层 x[p:p+L)、位置 p 的 h 和 ring、块长 C≥1、固定块参数\n输出：本层全部输出、末 h 和 ring，或失败\n1 核对身份、位置和维度；保留旧完整状态，候选工作区私有\n2 若 L=0：返回空输出和原状态副本\n3 按不超过 C 的长度依次处理各块：\n    批量计算本块 Norm、u 和 z_gate\n    用入口 ring 与块内较早的 u 计算每个位置的因果卷积\n    由卷积激活 v 产生 B、C、Δ，形成 alpha 和 beta\n    以入口 h 调用 AffinePrefixScan，得到本块各位置 h_t\n    对每个位置完成 C·h_t、skip、输出门、W_out 和残差\n    候选入口 h←本块末状态\n    候选入口 ring←原 ring 连接本块 u 后的末 K_c−1 项；K_c=1 时为空\n4 核\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:10998]\nhing 标为未完成工作。这是该历史设计的条件和限制，不能写成所有版本的统一行为。\n\n独立池、同字节池和大块子分配都可合法。统一页减少分配尺寸种类，却可能增加 padding、页尾浪费、检查点内部碎片和分叉复制粒度。页字节相等也不能证明各族支持同一个恢复位置。\n\n考虑如下设定轨迹，所有位置均为消费长度：\n\n| 事件 | 必须保留和取得的状态 | 推进与成本 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 目标恢复到 1024 | 全 KV 保存 [0,1024)，递归检查点为 {0,512,768}，窗口保存 [640,1024)，W＝128，卷积历史可取得 | 512 缺少所需窗口；768 合法；1024 缺递归快照，所以共同直接集合为 {0,768} |\n| 从 768 重放 | 保护 S(768)，全 KV 只暴露到 768，窗口暴露 [641,768)，可变状态复制到私有区 | 重放 256 个确定输入，完整执行所有混合层；不能只补递归层 |\n| 验证 4 个候选 | 从 S(1024) 出发，保存起点以及采用策略所需的候选边界 | 最终验证状态为 S(1028)，尚未成为正式状态 |\n| 仅接受前 2 个 | 取得准确 S(1026)，并对齐 grammar、随机与输出边界 | 可选保存候选快照，或从 S(1024) 重放两步；改长度不成立 |\n| 迁移 S(1026) | 冻结共同完成位置，或让后续写转入另一版本；目标取得同位置的全部族 | 目标就绪并取得提交权后才接管；源副本的释放还须等待在途使用结束 |\n\n窗口回滚也可能需要恢复。若物理窗口环有 128 槽，S(1024) 保存 [896,1024)，验证四步后保留 [900,1028)。从 1026 继续需要 [899,1026)，位置 899 已被覆盖。缩小 KV 有效长度无法重建它；应恢复窗口快照，或从受保护起点重放。\n\n若 B_S＝4.375 MiB，额外保存四个递归加卷积候选包需要 17.5 MiB，另计窗口、KV、输出及暂存；只保留起点则支付接受后重放。一般接受 a∈[0,γ]：a＝0 使用起点，a＝γ 可以使用最终候选，其余使用准确中间快照或重放。未被消费的修正或 bonus token 仍是 pending，不能提前计入 S 的消费长度。\n\n迁移覆盖式状态必须复制稳定版本。逐层复制时若源仍原地推进，目标可能取得“某层 1026、另一层 1027”的混合包。目标缺层、表示不符或中途失败时不发布；源保留完整恢复责任。取消和错误通知本身不证明 DMA 或 GPU 访问已经停止，旧访问排空前不能把页或暂存交给新请求。代次核验能拒绝迟到通知，却不能阻止已经发出的旧写覆盖复用地址。\n\n## 条件化资源账与方案反转\n\n以一次混合 decode 迭代、B 个请求各推进一个位置为范围。为简化乘 B，先假设同一层的请求具有相同历史长度；各请求长度不同时，应改为逐请求求和。L_l 为该层推进前可见的旧历史长度，W_l 包含当前 query，c_l 是该注意力层每个旧位置 K 与 V 合计的字节数。历史读取主项为\n\nM_read≈M_weight_read＋Σ_full B·L_l·c_l＋Σ_window B·min(L_l,W_l−1)·c_l＋Σ_rec B·(D_lN_l·s_h,l＋s_conv_read,l)＋M_metadata。\n\n各项单位均为本次迭代的字节数。s_h,l 仍是一个状态元素的字节数，D_lN_l·s_h,l 是每请求该层一次推进读取的完整旧 h。s_conv_read,l 定义为每请求该层一次推进读取的旧卷积历史字节数，不包括当前新输入、卷积权重和写回。对每个旧历史元素恰读一次的直接深度卷积，s_conv_read,l＝D_l(K_c,l−1)·s_u,l；K_c,l＝1 时为零。若实现重复装载历史或物化移位副本，按实际访问另加交通。\n\nM_weight_read 是本次迭代权重的读取量，不能在已经包含批量复用的情况下再统一乘 B；M_metadata 是本次迭代元数据读取总量。各层求和应识别实际共享对象与别名。该式是逻辑访问账，不是 DRAM 事务测量；物理共享前缀减少容量，却不保证多个 query 的历史读取按同样比例减少。寄存器、共享存储或 cache 命中可以改变 HBM 流量；没有驻留与事务证据时不能把这份逻辑账直接写成设备实测。\n\n新 KV、完整递归状态写回、卷积写入、快照复制和临时展开分别另计。若每步完整读写 h，递归 h 的读写合计为 Σ_rec 2BD_lN_l·s_h,l，与前文单层 2BDN·s_h 一致。环形卷积历史每步可只写 D_l 个新元素和槽位元数据；移位组织还可能复制旧项。状态包容量 B_S、一次旧状态读取和一次读写合计是三个不同的量，不能互相替代。\n\n独立核对可直接从坐标计数。单请求、单递归层 D＝2048、N＝16、s_h＝2 字节时，有 32,768 个状态元素；逐个元素读一次共 65,536 字节＝64 KiB，完整写回再付 64 KiB，h 的读写合计 128 KiB。若 K_c＝4、s_u＝2 字节，旧卷积历史有 3×2048 个元素，读取 12,288 字节＝12 KiB，故本层旧状态加卷积历史的读取为 76 KiB。环形组织写入当前新历史另需 4 KiB，以上状态与历史的读写主项合计为 144 KiB，尚未包含投影、系数、输出、权重和元数据。\n\n错误地使用 B·s_h 会把上述 h 读取计成 2 字节，低估 32,768 倍。若另设一个全注意力层旧历史长 64、每位置 KV 合计 512 字节，其历史读取是 32 KiB：正确账中递归 h 读取为它的两倍，错误账却会得到递归状态读取几乎可忽略的判断。这是逻辑字节比较，不能单凭它判定真实延迟瓶颈；还需比较计算、实际事务、有效带宽、并行度及固定开销。D 或 N 加倍、存储元素从 2 字节改为 4 字节，均应使 h 读取加倍；只增加历史长度不会增加这项。D＝N＝1 的退化算例无法区分“单元素”与“整层状态”，所以检查必须包括非退化维度。\n\n恢复和迁移还需要六类成本同时成立：计算含重放所有层及输出；访存含快照读取、目标写入、COW、状态更新与临时量；通信仅在跨设备或主机实际发生时计入；准备含位置交集、身份核对、分配和描述构造；同步含稳定快照、传输完成、层间依赖和接管；驻留含源、目标、候选和 staging 同时存活的物理并集。预留转成目标页仍是同一容量，不能重复相加。\n\n例如只迁移 4.375 MiB 状态包、有效链路为 8 GiB/s 时，纯载荷时间下界为约 0.534 ms；它不包含其他混合状态、准备、转换和接管。串行恢复可比较 T_prepare＋T_transfer＋T_replay＋T_publish；允许流水重叠时必须按依赖图求关键路径，并考虑链路与 HBM 的竞争，不能直接把所有时间相加或取一个无条件最大值。\n\n条件变化应沿依赖重新选择：\n\n- 固定 P 并保留所有周期快照时，历史越长，快照容量也线性增长。若注意力每 token 总字节为 c_KV，快照与全 KV 的渐近容量比约为 B_S/(P·c_KV\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 12000:14446]\n | 把元素字节当成整层字节；重复装载或 spill；准备与启动支配 | 先以 D×N 坐标及独立 dtype 宽度计数，再分别改变 D、N、存储精度和历史长；若修正逻辑账后设备交通仍偏大，查重复读取、事务及 spill；若交通已匹配而时间仍高，查计算、同步与固定开销 |\n| 融合或大块更慢 | HBM 物化减少但 spill 增加；驻留下降；同步或准备支配 | 分别记录实际临时字节、local 交通、资源、块数及时间线；未减少实际交通时不能继续用“省 HBM”解释收益 |\n\n交付检查的独立性来自仿射递推的乘积求和展开式、有理数精确算例，以及不使用环更新的完整因果卷积历史参考。负对照实际改变合成规则、块间 h/ring 和投机提交位置，并要求观测到错误；恢复资格另用显式读取集合核对范围公式。成本检查逐请求、逐状态坐标和逐历史项枚举读取，使用独立存储格式宽度对照聚合公式，并把遗漏 D×N 的旧式作为负对照；覆盖非退化维度、零 batch、单元素状态、无旧卷积历史及异构层求和。\n\n这些只是小规模 CPU 算术和有限状态模型。检查不证明真实 checkpoint 的前向实现、GPU 数值、跨 stream/NIC 可见性、异步回收安全、性能或知识对 Agent 的使用效果；上述设备诊断仍需对应实现和真实观测。交付脚本的实际执行状态以本轮检查记录为准，正文中的检查设计不代替执行证据。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-MAMBA：Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces](../sources/S-MAMBA.md)：固定 v2 的 §2 定义连续、离散与对角结构 SSM；§3.1–3.2 和 Algorithm 2 解释输入相关的 Δ、B、C 及其轴；§3.4 的块图和 §3.6 的低秩 Δ 投影支持本章对计算图来源的区分。§3.3 与附录 D 解释离散化、扫描及 C ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nYuhui Li♠\nFangyun Wei‡\nChao Zhang♠\nHongyang Zhang♣†\n♠Peking University\n‡Microsoft Research\n♣University of Waterloo\n†Vector Institute\nhongyang.zhang@uwaterloo.ca\nhttps://github.com/SafeAILab/EAGLE\nFigure 1: Speedup ratio of Vicuna and LLaMA2-Chat inference latency on the MT-bench for greedy (temperature=0)\nsettings. Speedup ratio of Medusa and Lookahead are copied from their original technical reports. With speculative sampling,\nthere is a lack of suitable draft models to accelerate the 7B model. Employing a 7B model as the draft model for a 13B\nmodel results in slow speeds due to the high overhead of the 7B model, rendering it less efficient than vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding. These scenarios are marked as N/A. In this paper, we only compare methods that do not need to finetune the\nbackbone models, ensuring the output text distribution remains constant.\nAbstract\nAuto-regressive decoding makes the inference of\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) time-consuming.\nWe propose a simple framework, EAGLE (Extrap-\nolation Algorithm for Greater Language-model\nEfficiency), for lossless acceleration. Unlike tra-\nditional speculative sampling methods, EAGLE\noperates the drafting process auto-regressively at\nthe more regular (second-top-layer) feature level\nand addresses the sampling uncertainty issues in\nthe next-feature prediction problems by integrat-\ning tokens from one time step ahead. The acceler-\nation provided by EAGLE is lossless: it involves\nno fine-tuning of the target LLM, and the gener-\nated text maintains the same distribution as that\nof vanilla auto-regressive decoding. As of the sub-\nmission of this paper, EAGLE is the fastest known\nframework within the speculative sampling family.\nOn MT-bench, EAGLE is 3x faster than vanilla de-\ncoding, 2x faster than Lookahead, and 1.6x faster\nthan Medusa. Using gpt-fast, EAGLE attains on\naverage 160 tokens/s with LLaMA2-Chat 13B on\na single RTX 3090 GPU, compared to 24 tokens/s\nof Huggingface’s implementations.\n1. Introduction\nAuto-regressive decoding has become the de facto standard\nfor large language models (LLMs). This process gener-\nates output tokens one at a time, which makes the genera-\ntion by LLMs both costly and slow. Speculative sampling\n(Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a) based methods\noffer a solution to this challenge. They divide the generation\nprocess of LLMs into two stages: the draft stage, where\npotential tokens are conjectured at a low cost, and the veri-\nfication stage, where these tokens are validated in parallel\nthrough a single forward pass of the LLM. The paralleliza-\ntion in speculative sampling facilitates the generation of\n1\narXiv:2401.15077v1  [cs.LG]  26 Jan 2024\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nmultiple post-check tokens per LLM forward pass, signifi-\ncantly enhancing speed. More importantly, the verification\nstage in speculative sampling ensures that the text distribu-\ntion aligns precisely with the decoding results of the original\nLLM, maintaining the integrity of the generated content.\nThe key to applying speculative sampling is to identify a\ndraft model that is sufficiently similar to the original LLM\nyet has lower latency. Speculative sampling typically em-\nploys a lower-parameter version of a LLM from the same\nseries as the draft model. For instance, in the LLaMA2\n(Touvron et al., 2023) series which includes models with\n7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, using the 7B model as a\nproxy of the 70B model is valid, while finding a suitable\ndraft model for the smallest 7B variant is tricky. An alter-\nnative could be to use TinyLLaMA (Zhang et al., 2024),\nbut this is not feasible for instruct-tuned models due to the\ninconsistency in instruction templates between LLaMA2-\nChat and TinyLLaMA-Chat. For 13B and 70B models, a 7B\nmodel within the same series could serve as the draft model.\nHowever, due to the high overhead of the 7B model, the\nacceleration effect of speculative sampling is not optimal.\nTraining a new, appropriately sized draft model specifically\nfor speculative sampling is not an ideal solution either due\nto the high cost: TinyLLaMA is trained on 3,000B tokens,\nwhereas EAGLE is trained on 2-4B tokens.\nThe key to enhancing acceleration in speculative sampling\nlies in reducing the time overhead and improving the ac-\nceptance rate of the draft by the original LLM (Chen et al.,\n2023b; Xia et al., 2023; Santilli et al., 2023). Numerous ap-\nproaches focus on reducing the overhead of the draft phase.\nLookahead (Fu et al., 2023) employs n-gram and Jacobi iter-\nation, while Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) utilizes a set of MLPs\nthat predict tokens based on the second-top-layer feature of\nthe original LLM. These strategies significantly decrease\nthe latency in generating drafts, leading to improved accel-\neration. However, the ultimate potential of these methods is\ncapped due to the lower accuracy of the drafts they produce.\nBuilding upon the observation that auto-regression at the\nfeature level is more manageable than at the token level,\nwe introduce a simple framework, EAGLE (Extrapolation\nAlgorithm for Greater Language-model Efficiency), which\ndiverges from direct token prediction and instead performs\nauto-regressive operations at the feature level. By incorpo-\nrating a token sequence advanced by one time step, EAGLE\ncircumvents the uncertainty associated with feature-level\nauto-regression. With the draft model comprising merely a\ntransformer decoder layer, EAGLE achieves a draft accuracy\nof approximately 0.8, substantially surpassing Medusa’s 0.6.\nWe evaluated EAGLE on the MT-bench (Zheng et al., 2023),\na highly realistic benchmark simulating actual applications\nand real-world scenarios, including multi-turn instructions\nakin to dialogues with ChatGPT. We have chosen to uti-\nFigure 2: Speedup ratio on the MT-bench for non-greedy\n(temperature=1) settings. Lookahead is confined to greedy\ndecoding, and the non-greedy generation of Medusa does\nnot guarantee lossless performance. Therefore, EAGLE is\nnot compared with these methods.\nlize this benchmark as it has been employed by the cur-\nrent state-of-the-art, including Lookahead and Medusa, to\ndemonstrate their speedup ratios. This choice facilitates\na fair and direct comparison between our approach and\nthese benchmarks. Under a greedy decoding setting, for\nVicuna-13B and LLaMA2-Chat 13B, 70B, EAGLE offers a\n3x acceleration that is theoretically guaranteed to maintain\ntext distribution of the original LLM and is ready to use\nright out–of-the-box. Compared to the recently proposed\nspeculative sampling-based frameworks, Lookahead and\nMedusa, EAGLE achieves 2x and 1.6x speedups, respec-\ntively. While enhancing speed, EAGLE also increases the\nthroughput of LLM systems by 2x. EAGLE operates in\nparallel with other acceleration or throughput-improving\nmethods, such as quantization, compilation, etc. Combining\nEAGLE with these techniques could further reduce the oper-\national costs of LLM systems. For example, with gpt-fast1,\nEAGLE accelerates LLaMA2-Chat 7B decoding from 24.5\ntokens/s to 160.4 tokens/s on a single RTX 3090 GPU.\nEAGLE boasts low training costs. For the LLaMA2-Chat\n70B model, EAGLE trains a decoder layer with fewer than\n1B parameters using no more than 70k dialogues from the\nShareGPT dataset. The training is completed in 1-2 days\non 4x A100 (40G) GPUs. In practical applications, EAGLE\nrequires only a single training session to provide accelera-\ntion for each query. As the number of queries increases, the\namortized training cost of EAGLE becomes negligible.\nCompared with existing speculative sampling-based tech-\nniques, the advantages of EAGLE include:\n• Simplicity: EAGLE is a new framework to accelerate\nauto-regressive decoding and is applicable to any auto-\nregressive LLMs. The method adds only a lightweight\nplug-in (a single transformer decoder layer) to the\nLLM, which can be easily deployed in a production\n1https://github.com/pytorch-labs/gpt-fast\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nenvironment. In this paper, we have applied EAGLE to\nLLaMA2-Chat (7B, 13B, 70B), Vicuna (7B, 13B, 33B)\nand Mixtral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1 in a zero-shot way on\nthe MT-bench and GSM8K datasets.\n• Reliability: EAGLE does not involve any fine-tuning\nof the original LLM, and the preservation of the output\ndistribution by EAGLE is theoretically guaranteed for\nboth the greedy and non-greedy settings. This ensures\nno risk of degradation in edge cases, where acceleration\nmight lead to erroneous or even harmful LLM outputs.\nThis is in sharp contrast to Lookahead and Medusa\nwhich focuses on greedy settings only.\n• Speed: EAGLE stands out as the fastest framework\nwithin the family of speculative sampling as of the\nsubmission of this paper. Notably, on the Vicuna and\nLLaMA2-Chat for greedy inference (see Figure 1), EA-\nGLE surpasses vanilla decoding speeds by a factor of\n3, outpaces Lookahead decoding by 2 times, and is\n1.6 times faster than Medusa decoding. Using gpt-fast,\nEAGLE attains on average 160 tokens/s with LLaMA2-\nChat 13B on a single RTX 3090 GPU, compared to\n24 tokens/s of Huggingface’s implementations. In the\nMoE (Mixture of Experts) model, specifically the Mix-\ntral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1 (Jiang et al., 2024), the EAGLE\nalgorithm achieves a speed increase of 1.5 times.\nIn addition to introducing the simple yet efficient frame-\nwork of EAGLE, we also provide an analysis of the factors\ncontributing to its effectiveness, which might be of indepen-\ndent interest to other speculative sampling methods (Section\n5.3.2). EAGLE is built upon the following two observations:\n• Firstly, utilizing top-layer features proves to be more\neffective than employing bottom-layer token embed-\ndings when using the same lightweight network.\n• Secondly, the uncertainty inherent in the sampling pro-\ncess significantly constrains the performance of draft\nmodels that only input top-layer features. Hence, in-\ncorporating the sampling results (token) into the draft\nmodel is crucial.\n2. Preliminaries\nNotations. In this paper, we refer to the Large Language\nModel intended for acceleration as the “target LLM” and the\nmodel used for rapid draft generation as the “draft model”.\nUnless specified otherwise, “feature” refers to the second-\nto-top-layer feature of LLM, that is, the hidden state before\nthe LM Head. We denote tokens by lowercase letters t, their\nembeddings by e, and features by f, and the distribution\nby p. We use uppercase letters to represent sequences. For\ninstance, Ti:j denotes the sequence (ti, ti+1, . . . , tj). Con-\nsidering a LLM, given an input token sequence T1:j, the\nLLM acquires corresponding embeddings E1:j through the\nembedding layer. These are processed through multiple de-\ncoder layers to yield features F1:j. The LM Head then maps\nfj onto a distribution pj+1 = LM Head(fj), from which\nthe next token tj+1 is sampled. As an example, vanilla\nauto-regression at the token level can be expressed by the\nprocess (T1:j →E1:j →fj →pj+1 →tj+1) for any\ninteger j ≥1.\nSpeculative sampling. Speculative sampling alternates be-\ntween the draft and verification phases. Consider the current\nsequence T1:j. During the draft phase, speculative sam-\npling employs a model, smaller than the target LLM, to\nauto-regressively generate γ tokens ˆTj+1:j+γ,2 and records\nthe corresponding distributions ˆPj+1:j+γ. In the verifica-\ntion phase, a single forward pass of the target LLM yields\nthe probabilities for the corresponding positions. During\nthe verification phase, a single forward pass of the target\nLLM provides the probabilities for the corresponding po-\nsitions. Tokens are then sequentially evaluated for accep-\ntance, with token ˆtj+i having an acceptance probability\nmin(1, pj+i/ˆpj+i). Upon the rejection of a token ˆtj+i, all\nsubsequent tokens are discarded, and this token is resam-\npled based on a distribution norm(max(0, pj+i −ˆpj+i)).\nAppendix A.1 of speculative sampling (Leviathan et al.,\n2023) proves that the aforementioned sampling method is\nequivalent to directly sampling from the distribution of the\ntarget LLM. EAGLE also employs this sampling approach,\nensuring that the distribution of the generated text re-\nmains unchanged for both the greedy and non-greedy\nsettings.\n3. Observations\nWe begin with two observations that directly motivate the\ndesign of EAGLE.\n3.1. Auto-regressing features is easier than tokens\nToken sequences represent simple transformations of nat-\nural language and are inherently complex. In contrast, the\nsecond-top-layer features of LLMs, after undergoing a lin-\near transformation (LM Head), can predict the next token,\nmaking the feature sequences more structured and regu-\nlar. Consequently, performing auto-regression at the feature\nlevel is expected to be more manageable than at the token\nlevel. We hypothesize this is because high-level feature’s\nevolution path over time is simpler than that of tokens, thus\nthe former can be characterized by a smaller model. To\nvalidate this hypothesis, we designed experiments on MT-\n2We will frequently use the hat notations to denote the corre-\nsponding terms for the draft models.\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nFigure 3: Accuracy and speedup ratio of draft models based\non tokens and features, tested on MT-bench with Vicuna 7B\nas the target LLM.\nbench with Vicuna 7B (Chiang et al., 2023) by setting the\ndraft models as a one-layer transformer decoder, for both\nthe feature and token auto-regression. As illustrated in Fig-\nure 3, auto-regressive feature processing followed by token\ngeneration ((f1, f2) →f3 →t4) using the frozen LM Head\nyields a much higher accuracy compared to auto-regressive\ntoken processing ((t1, t2, t3) →t4), with an improvement\nas large as 30%. This results in a much higher speedup ratio\ntoo (1.5x→1.9x).\n3.2. Uncertainty matters in next-feature predictions\nWhen generating text, the target LLM does not directly\npredict tokens; instead, it forecasts the probability distri-\nbution of tokens and then samples based on this distribu-\ntion, thereby introducing randomness into the generation\nprocess. Unlike tokens, features are high-dimensional and\ncontinuous, precluding the use of the same sampling-based\napproach. Consider the example illustrated in Figure 4. If\n“am” is sampled, the feature sequence becomes (fI, fam).\nConversely, if “always” is sampled, the feature sequence\nbecomes (fI, falways). This introduces ambiguity into the\nauto-regressive model of feature sequences: it is uncertain\nwhether the next feature following fI should be fam or\nfalways. Medusa also confronts this issue. Its objective is\nto predict the probability of tokens spaced by an interval,\nsuch as using fI to predict pam or palways in this example,\ncorresponding to the probability of the next token following\n“I am” or “I always”, respectively. This inherent uncertainty\nrenders it impossible for Medusa’s draft model to perfectly\nemulate the target LLM. To address this issue, EAGLE in-\nputs the token sequence from one time step ahead, which\nincludes the sampling outcomes, into the draft model. In\nthe example illustrated in Figure 4, this involves predicting\nfalways based on fI and talways, and predicting fam based\non fI and tam. As illustrated in Figure 5, by addressing the\nuncertainty, the speedup ratio further increases from 1.9x to\n2.8x.\n4. EAGLE\nEAGLE is built upon the above two observations. It encom-\npasses both a drafting phase and a verification phase, to be\nI\n𝑒I\n𝑓I\n𝑝(am)=0.6 \n𝑝(always)=0.4 \nsampling\nam\n𝑒am\n𝑓am\n𝑝(excited)=0.3 \n𝑝(ready)=0.7 \nsampling\nalways\n𝑒always\n𝑓always\n𝑝(begin)=0.8 \n𝑝(look)=0.2 \n𝑝always\n𝑝I\n𝑝am\nFigure 4: Uncertainty in feature sequences. The next fea-\nture following fI is contingent on the sampling outcome\nand cannot be determined solely based on fI, where both\n“always” and “am” are possible to follow the token “I” and\nlead to two branches.\nFigure 5: Accuracy and speedup ratio of draft models based\non feature and feature&shifted-token. The feature&shifted-\ntoken approach effectively resolves the uncertainty. The\nmodels were tested on MT-bench, employing Vicuna 7B as\nthe target LLM.\nconsistent with other speculative sampling based methods.\n4.1. Drafting phase\nThe primary distinction between EAGLE and other methods\nlies predominantly in the drafting phase. Figure 6 illustrates\na schematic of the drafting phase for different methods.\nSpeculative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al.,\n2023a) and Lookahead (Fu et al., 2023) predict tokens based\non tokens. Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) independently predicts\nt4 and t5 using the feature f2 from the target LLM. EAGLE\npredicts f3 using the feature sequence (f1, f2) and the token\nsequence (t2, t3), advanced by one time step. From p4 =\nLM Head(f3), t4 is sampled. Subsequently, f3 and t4 are\nconcatenated into the input sequence to predict the next\nfeature f4 and sample the subsequent token t5.\nAs illustrated in Figure 7, EAGLE’s draft model comprises\nthree modules: the Embedding layer, LM Head, and Auto-\nregression Head. The Embedding layer and LM Head em-\nploy the parameters of the original LLM and do not neces-\nsitate additional training. The draft model takes as input a\nfeature sequence of shape (bs, seq len, hidden dim) and an\nadvanced token sequence of shape (bs, seq len). It then con-\nverts the token sequence into a token embedding sequence\nof shape (bs, seq len, hidden dim), and concatenates it to\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n𝑡1\n𝑡2\n𝑡3\nSmaller LLM\n𝑡4\n𝑡1\n𝑡2\n𝑡3\nSmaller LLM\n𝑡5\n𝑡4\nSpeculative Sampling\n𝑡3\n2-Gram, Jacobi\n𝑡4\n2-Gram, Jacobi\n𝑡5\n𝑡4\nLookahead\nMedusa\nEmbedding layer &\nAuto-regression Head\n𝑓3\n𝑓4\nEAGLE\n𝑡4\n𝑡5\n𝑓1\n𝑡2\n𝑓2\n𝑡3\n𝑓2\n𝑡3\n𝑓3\n𝑡4\n𝑓1\n𝑡2\nMedusa Head1\n𝑡4\n𝑓2\n𝑡5\nMedusa Head2\nEmbedding layer &\nAuto-regression Head\nFigure 6: A comparison of the methods for drafting the\nfourth and fifth tokens, t4 and t5, where (t1, t2) is the\nprompt. t (represented by blue blocks) denotes tokens, and\nf (orange blocks) signifies the features, with subscripts in-\ndicating their positions in the sequence. Finally, the predicted fea-\nture and the sampled token are concatenated into the input,\nfacilitating the continuation of the auto-regressive process.\nEAGLE generates a tree-structured draft. To enhance effi-\nciency, we implement tree attention, enabling the creation\nof a draft tree with a depth of m through m forward passes,\nthereby encompassing more than m tokens. In the example\nillustrated in Figure 7, EAGLE drafts a tree of 10 tokens\nthrough 3 forward passes.\n4.2. Training of the draft models\nPredicting the next feature constitutes a regression task, for\nwhich we employ Smooth L1 loss (see Figure 6 EAGLE):\nE2:i+1 = Token Embedding(T2:i+1),\nˆfi+1 = Auto-regression Head(E2:i+1, F1:i),\nLreg = Smooth L1(fi+1, ˆfi+1).\nPredicting features is an intermediary objective of the draft\nmodel, with the ultimate goal being the prediction of tokens\nto generate a sequence of tokens. Conse-\nquently, we set wcls to 0.1.\nThe optimal approach for training the Auto-regression Head\ninvolves generating text auto-regressively using the target\nLLM. However, such data generation is costly. Fortunately,\nEAGLE exhibits low sensitivity to training data (ablation\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nstudy in Section 5.3.3). Instead of employing text generated\nby the target LLM, we utilize a fixed dataset, substantially\nreducing the overhead. During the drafting phase, EAGLE\nauto-regressively processes features. Inaccuracies in fea-\ntures can lead to error accumulation. To mitigate this is-\nsue, we employ data augmentation by adding random noise\nsampled from a uniform distribution U(−0.1, 0.1) to the\nsecond-top-layer feature vectors of the target LLM during\ntraining (Jain et al., 2023).\n4.3. Verification phase\nEmploying tree attention, the target LLM computes the\nprobability of each token in the tree-structured draft through\na single forward pass. At every level of the draft tree, we\nrecursively apply speculative sampling algorithms to sample\nor adjust the distribution, consistent with SpecInfer (Miao\net al., 2023) and SpecTr (Sun et al., 2023), ensuring that\nthe distribution of the output text aligns with that of the\noriginal LLM. Simultaneously, we record the accepted to-\nkens and their corresponding features, which serve as the\nstarting point for auto-regressive feature extrapolation in the\nsubsequent draft phase.\n5. Experiments\nModels and tasks. We conducted experiments on Vicuna\nmodels (with sizes of 7B, 13B, 33B), LLaMA2-chat models\n(7B, 13B, 70B) and Mixtral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1, encom-\npassing the common sizes of current mainstream Large\nLanguage Models (LLMs). Our evaluations span dialogue\nand mathematical reasoning tasks. For dialogue tasks, we\nutilized the MT-bench, which comprises highly realistic sit-\nuational questions designed to test a model’s capabilities in\nmulti-turn conversations and adherence to instructions. For\nmathematical reasoning tasks, we employed the GSM8K\n(Cobbe et al., 2021) dataset, consisting of high-quality grade\nschool math problems created by human problem writers.\nSpeculative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023) conducted\nexperiments with a batch size of 1, a setting subsequently\nadopted by other works such as DistillSpec (Zhou et al.,\n2023) and BiLD (Kim et al., 2023). Similarly, the majority\nof our experiments also adopted this setting. Experiments\nwith a batch size greater than 1 are presented in Section 5.4.\nMetrics. Similar to other methods based on speculative\nsampling, EAGLE primarily focuses on latency rather than\nthroughput. Following the methodology of DistillSpec, we\nassess acceleration effects using the following metrics:\n• Walltime speedup ratio: The actual test speedup ratio\nrelative to vanilla auto-regressive decoding.\n• Average acceptance length τ: The average number of\ntokens accepted per forward pass of the original LLM.\nFigure 8: Speedup ratios of EAGLE across various tasks.\n• Acceptance rate α: The ratio of the number of tokens\naccepted to the number of tokens generated during the\ndraft phase. The acceptance rate metric measures the\naccuracy of drafts. For tree draft, multiple tokens may\nbe sampled at the same location, yet only one token\nis accepted, rendering the acceptance rate metric un-\nsuitable for assessment in this context. Therefore, for\nthe experiments reported in this paper concerning ac-\nceptance rate, we do not use tree draft but rather chain\ndraft, similar to speculative sampling and DistillSpec.\nThe draft model of EAGLE takes feature sequence and\na token sequence as input. The auto-regressive pro-\ncessing of features in the draft phase can lead to the\npropagation of errors. We define the Acceptance rate\nwhen the input contains n features predicted by the\ndraft model, which may include errors, as n-α.\nAcceleration of EAGLE theoretically guarantees the preser-\nvation of the original LLMs’ output distribution. Conse-\nquently, evaluating the quality of EAGLE’s generated results\nis both unnecessary and meaningless.\nTraining. We fixed the target LLMs. EAGLE was trained on\nthe ShareGPT platform, utilizing 68,000 dialogue iterations\nwith a learning rate set at 3e-5. We employed the AdamW\noptimizer with beta values (β1, β2) set to (0.9, 0.95) and\nimplemented gradient clipping of 0.5. The trainable parame-\nters of EAGLE corresponding to the 7B, 13B, 33B, and 70B\nmodels are 0.24B, 0.37B, 0.56B, and 0.99B, respectively.\nThe trainable parameters of EAGLE for MoE model Mixtral\n8x7B is 0.28B. EAGLE is characterized by its low training\ncost; the Auto-regression Head is trainable within 1-2 days\non an A100 40G server for the 70B models.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n5.1. Effectiveness\nFigure 1 illustrates the speedup ratios of EAGLE, Medusa,\nand Lookahead on the MT-bench, with temperature=0. On\nthe Vicuna 13B and LLaMA2-Chat 13B, 70B models, EA-\nGLE achieves a speed that is 3x faster than vanilla auto-\nregressive decoding, 2x faster than Lookahead, and 1.6x\nfaster than Medusa. Figure 2 and Table 2 showcase the\nacceleration ratios of EAGLE under various settings. Fig-\nure 8 presents the speedup ratios of EAGLE across vari-\nous tasks. The coding task, which involves a substantial\nnumber of fixed templates, exhibits the most significant\nspeedup effect. EAGLE also demonstrates notable effec-\ntiveness in other tasks. Conversely, standard decoder-only models ne-\ncessitate reading all weights, regardless of whether one or\nmultiple tokens are forwarded.\n5.2. Case study: EAGLE + gpt-fast\nEAGLE is compatible with other acceleration technologies.\nWe conducted experiments combining EAGLE with gpt-fast,\nwhich employs quantization and compilation for accelera-\ntion. As shown in Figure 4, by integrating EAGLE with\ngpt-fast, we increased the generation speed of LLaMA2-\nChat 7B on a single RTX 3090 to 160.4 tokens/s, compared\nto 24.5 tokens/s using Huggingface’s implementations.\nTable 4: Generation speed of combining EAGLE with gpt-\nfast. Ablation study\n5.3.1. TREE ATTENTION\nEAGLE, similar to SpecInfer and Medusa, employs tree\nattention, where both the generation and validation of\ndrafts are tree-structured. In contrast, methods like specula-\ntive sampling do not use tree attention, resulting in chain-\nstructured draft generation and validation. Table 5 and Fig-\nure 9 present comparative results indicating the impact of\nusing tree attention. The implementation of tree draft and\nverification in EAGLE results in an approximate increase of\n0.6-0.8 in the average acceptance length and about 0.3-0.5\nin the speedup ratio. Compared to chain draft and verifica-\ntion, tree draft and verification do not increase the number\nof forward passes in the model (both the target LLM and\nthe draft model), but they do increase the number of tokens\nprocessed per forward pass. Consequently, the improvement\nin the speedup ratio is less pronounced than the increase\nin average acceptance length. Notably, even without em-\nploying tree draft and verification, EAGLE demonstrates a\nsignificant acceleration effect, approximately in the range\nof 2.2x-2.7x.\n5.3.2. INPUTS OF DRAFT MODELS\nCompared to other speculative sampling-based methods,\nthe key innovation of EAGLE lies in its utilization of fea-\ntures computed by the target LLM and the incorporation\nof sampling outcomes into the input of the draft model\nto address randomness. We conducted an ablation study\non Vicuna 7B, assessing draft models with varying inputs.\nWe tested four types of inputs: feature&shifted-token (EA-\nGLE), feature&unshifted-token, token, and feature. Both\nfeature&shifted-token (EAGLE) and feature&unshifted-\ntoken integrate semantic information at different levels. The\ndistinction lies in the fact that feature&shifted-token (EA-\nGLE) inputs tokens advanced by one time step, equipping\nit to address randomness effectively. Apart from the use of\na FC layer to reduce dimensionality for the feature&token\ninput, the structure of the draft model remains entirely con-\nsistent. Figure 10 presents the experimental outcomes on\nthe MT-bench with Vicuna 7B as the target LLM. Three\nobservations can be drawn.\n• First, when the number of parameters of the draft\nmodel is limited, utilizing features yields slightly better\nresults than tokens.\n• Second, integrating features and tokens slightly en-\nhances performance. This is primarily because discrete,\nerror-free tokens mitigate the accumulation of errors in\nfeatures (as evidenced by the near-equivalence of the\nacceptance rate of the feature&unshifted-token draft\nmodel to that of the feature-only model, 0-α, but with\na markedly higher 1-α).\n• Third, addressing the randomness inherent in the sam-\npling process results in the most significant improve-\nment. The feature&shifted-token scheme, compared\nto feature&unshifted-token, adds no complexity yet\nmarkedly enhances the draft model’s capability by sim-\nply advancing the token by one time step, allowing the\ndraft model to account for the randomness in sampling.\n5.3.3. TRAINING DATA\nEAGLE utilizes a fixed dataset for training. Employing the\ntarget LLM for auto-regressive decoding on dataset queries\nto generate training data may yield enhanced performance\nbut would substantially increase training overhead. We con-\nducted ablation experiments on the MT-bench dataset. As\nshown in Table 6, training the draft model with data gener-\nated by the target LLM leads to only a slight improvement\nin performance. This suggests that EAGLE is not sensitive\nto the training data, allowing for the adoption of a fixed\ndataset approach. Consequently, this choice significantly\nreduces the training costs of EAGLE.\n5.4. Batch size and throughput\nInference in LLMs is memory-bound (Patterson, 2004;\nShazeer, 2019), leaving GPU computational resources un-\nderutilized. The principle behind the speculative sampling-\nbased approach in enhancing generation speed lies in more\neffectively utilizing GPU computational resources. As the\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.5\n2.0\n2.5\nT = 0\nSpeedup\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n2\n3\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.4\n0.6\n0.8\n0-\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n1-\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.0\n1.5\n2.0\nT = 1\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.5\n2.0\n2.5\n3.0\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\nfeature&shifted-token\nfeature&unshifted-token\ntoken\nfeature\nFigure 10: Performance of draft models with varying inputs. The target LLM is Vicuna 7B, and the test dataset is MT-bench.\nSpeed refers to the walltime speedup ratio, τ denotes the average acceptance length, 0-α represents the acceptance rate with\nentirely precise inputs, 1-α indicates the acceptance rate when the input includes one imprecise feature, and T refers to the\ntemperature.\nTable 6: The speedup ratios and average acceptance length τ\nusing different training datasets evaluated on the MT-bench,\nwith the target LLM being LLaMA2-Chat 7B and the tem-\nperature set to 0. “Fixed dataset” refers to both questions\nand answers originating from the ShareGPT dataset. “Data\ngenerated by target LLM” denotes that while questions are\nsourced from the ShareGPT dataset, the answers are gener-\nated by the target LLM.\nTraining data\nSpeedup\nτ\nFixed dataset\n2.78x\n3.62\nData generated by target LLM\n2.88x\n3.75\nbatch size increases, the available computational capacity\nof the GPU decreases, leading to a reduction in the ac-\nceleration effect. In this section, we present experimental\nresults for scenarios where the batch size exceeds 1. As\ndemonstrated in Table 7, the speedup ratio diminishes with\nincreasing batch size. When using Vicuna 7B as the target\nLLM, the speedup ratio at bs=4 is higher than at bs=3. This\nis attributed to the fact that, during the verification phase\nof EAGLE, the target LLM processes multiple tokens in a\nsingle forward pass, and the processing at bs=4 is faster than\nat bs=3. In contrast, with vanilla auto-regressive decoding\nwhere the target LLM processes one token per forward pass,\nthe speeds at bs=3 and bs=4 are nearly identical.\nAlthough speculative sampling-based methods predomi-\nnantly focus on latency, we also investigated EAGLE’s\nthroughput for batch size > 1, another key metric for LLM\nsystems. Compared to vanilla auto-regressive decoding,\nEAGLE requires roughly the same CUDA memory. For\nVicuna 7B as the target LLM, operating under a memory\nconstraint of a single RTX 3090 with 24G of CUDA mem-\nory, the maximum batch size (bs) for vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding and EAGLE are 8 and 7, respectively. In the\ncase of LLaMA2-Chat 70B, constrained by 4 A100 (40G)\nGPUs totaling 160G of CUDA memory, the maximum bs\nfor vanilla auto-regressive decoding and EAGLE are 5 and\n4, respectively. All evaluations were conducted at FP16\nprecision. We calculated the throughput for different bs and\nselected the maximum value. Both vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding and EAGLE achieve maximum throughput at their\nrespective maximum bs. Tree attention consumes more\ncomputational resources. At a batch of bs=7, the computa-\ntional resources are less abundant, making the non-use of\ntree attention more advantageous. As illustrated in Table 7,\nEAGLE achieves a 2x increase in throughput.\n6. Related Work\nThere has been considerable research into accelerating lan-\nguage models, involving techniques such as distillation (Hin-\nton et al., 2015), quantization (Hubara et al., 2018). These\nmethods aim to reduce the latency per forward pass.\n9\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nTable 7: Speedup ratios at different batch sizes and through-\nput of EAGLE. The evaluation dataset is MT-bench, with\nthe temperature parameter set to 0.\nBatch size\n1\n2\n3\n4\nThroughput\nVicuna 7B\n2.90x\n2.87x\n2.65x\n2.76x\n1.97x\nLLaMA2-Chat 70B\n3.01x\n2.81x\n2.50x\n2.40x\n1.99x\nSimilar to our approach are frameworks based on specula-\ntive sampling. Early works (Stern et al., 2018; Sun et al.,\n2021) accelerated greedy decoding, while speculative sam-\npling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a) extended it\nto non-greedy sampling, provably maintaining the original\noutput distribution. Ensuring unchanged output distribution\nmakes acceleration more challenging; many studies have\nexplored lossy acceleration as a trade-off. For instance,\nDistillSpec (Zhou et al., 2023) modifies acceptance proba-\nbilities using a lenience function, BiLD (Kim et al., 2023)\naccepts drafts if the distance metric from the original LLM\ndistribution is below a certain threshold, and Medusa (Cai\net al., 2023) uses a minimum of a hard threshold and an\nentropy-dependent threshold for truncation. In contrast, EA-\nGLE does not employ any relaxations and maintains the\noutput distribution of the LLM unchanged.\nThe primary differences among speculative sampling-based\nmethods manifest predominantly in the draft phase. Specu-\nlative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a)\nutilizes a lower-parameter version of the original LLM as\nthe draft model. Self-Speculative Decoding (Zhang et al.,\n2023) skips some layers of the original LLM during draft\ngeneration. SpecInfer (Miao et al., 2023) employs a set of\nsmall models to generate drafts in parallel. Cascade Specu-\nlative Drafting (Chen et al., 2023b) and Staged Speculative\nDecoding (Spector & Re, 2023) cascade different overhead\ndraft models. Online Speculative Decoding (Liu et al., 2023)\ntrains the draft model on a distribution of queries. Methods\n(Hooper et al., 2023; Fu et al., 2023; Yang et al., 2023b) such\nas Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) do not employ a separate target\nLLM; instead, they generate drafts by utilizing features or\nweights from the target LLM. REST (He et al., 2023) gener-\nates drafts based on retrieval methods. LLMA (Yang et al.,\n2023a), used for tasks like grammatical correction where\ninput and output overlap, retrieves drafts directly from the\ninput.\n7. Conclusion\nIn this paper, we introduce EAGLE, an efficient framework\nfor speculative sampling. EAGLE conducts the drafting\nprocess auto-regressively at the more structured (second-\ntop-layer) feature level and mitigates sampling uncertainty\nin predicting the next feature by incorporating tokens from\none time step ahead. EAGLE is guaranteed to preserve the\noutput distribution of the LLM while significantly enhancing\ngeneration speed. On MT-bench, EAGLE is 3x faster than\nvanilla auto-regressive decoding, 2x faster than Lookahead,\nand 1.6x faster than Medusa.\nAcknowledgements. K., Ros-\ntamizadeh, A., Kumar, S., Kagy, J.-F., and Agarwal, R.\nDistillSpec: Improving speculative decoding via knowl-\nedge distillation. arXiv preprint arXiv:2310.08461, 2023.\n11\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 47854]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 47854, "selected_characters": 47854, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-SPEC/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "66ebddbde83df97b2389d7e0e98c844435478aedeec56d024bf52b8cc0d94cec", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "--- PAGE 1 ---\nFast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding\nYaniv Leviathan * 1 Matan Kalman * 1 Yossi Matias 1\nAbstract\nInference from large autoregressive models like\nTransformers is slow - decoding K tokens takes\nK serial runs of the model. In this work we in-\ntroduce speculative decoding - an algorithm to\nsample from autoregressive models faster without\nany changes to the outputs, by computing several\ntokens in parallel. At the heart of our approach lie\nthe observations that (1) hard language-modeling\ntasks often include easier subtasks that can be ap-\nproximated well by more efﬁcient models, and\n(2) using speculative execution and a novel sam-\npling method, we can make exact decoding from\nthe large models faster, by running them in par-\nallel on the outputs of the approximation mod-\nels, potentially generating several tokens concur-\nrently, and without changing the distribution. Our\nmethod can accelerate existing off-the-shelf mod-\nels without retraining or architecture changes. We\ndemonstrate it on T5-XXL and show a 2X-3X\nacceleration compared to the standard T5X imple-\nmentation, with identical outputs.\n1. Introduction\nLarge autoregressive models, notably large Transformers\n(Vaswani et al., 2017), are much more capable than smaller\nmodels, as is evidenced countless times in recent years e.g.,\nin the text or image domains, like GPT-3 (Brown et al.,\n2020), LaMDA (Thoppilan et al., 2022), Parti (Yu et al.,\n2022), and PaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022). Unfortunately,\na single decode step from these larger models is signiﬁcantly\nslower than a step from their smaller counterparts, and mak-\ning things worse, these steps are done serially - decoding K\ntokens takes K serial runs of the model.\nGiven the importance of large autoregressive models and\nspeciﬁcally large Transformers, several approaches were\n*Equal\ncontribution\n1Google\nResearch,\nMountain\nView,\nCA,\nUSA.\nCorrespondence\nto:\nYaniv\nLeviathan\n<leviathan@google.com>.\nProceedings of the 40 th International Conference on Machine\nLearning, Honolulu, Hawaii, USA. PMLR 202, 2023. Copyright\n2023 by the author(s).\ndeveloped to make inference from them faster. Some ap-\nproaches aim to reduce the inference cost for all inputs\nequally (e.g. Hinton et al., 2015; Jaszczur et al., 2021;\nHubara et al., 2016; So et al., 2021; Shazeer, 2019). Other\napproaches stem from the observation that not all infer-\nence steps are born alike - some require a very large model,\nwhile others can be approximated well by more efﬁcient\nmodels. These adaptive computation methods (e.g. Han\net al., 2021; Sukhbaatar et al., 2019; Schuster et al., 2021;\nScardapane et al., 2020; Bapna et al., 2020; Elbayad et al.,\n2019; Schwartz et al., 2020) aim to use less compute re-\nsources for easier inference steps. While many of these\nsolutions have proven extremely effective in practice, they\nusually require changing the model architecture, changing\nthe training-procedure and re-training the models, and don’t\nmaintain identical outputs.\nThe key observation above, that some inference steps are\n“harder” and some are “easier”, is also a key motivator for\nour work. We additionally observe that inference from large\nmodels is often not bottlenecked on arithmetic operations,\nbut rather on memory bandwidth and communication, so\nadditional computation resources might be available. There-\nfore we suggest increasing concurrency as a complemen-\ntary approach to using an adaptive amount of computation.\nSpeciﬁcally, we are able to accelerate inference without\nchanging the model architectures, without changing the\ntraining-procedures or needing to re-train the models, and\nwithout changing the model output distribution. This is\naccomplished via speculative execution.\nSpeculative execution (Burton, 1985; Hennessy & Patterson,\n2012) is an optimization technique, common in processors,\nwhere a task is performed in parallel to verifying if it’s\nactually needed - the payoff being increased concurrency.\nA well-known example of speculative execution is branch\nprediction. For speculative execution to be effective, we\nneed an efﬁcient mechanism to suggest tasks to execute\nthat are likely to be needed. In this work, we generalize\nspeculative execution to the stochastic setting - where a\ntask might be needed with some probability. Applying this\nto decoding from autoregressive models like Transformers,\nwe sample generations from more efﬁcient approximation\nmodels as speculative preﬁxes for the slower target mod-\nels. With a novel sampling method, speculative sampling,\nwe maximize the probability of these speculative tasks to\n1\narXiv:2211.17192v2  [cs.LG]  18 May 2023\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nFast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding\nFigure 1. Our technique illustrated in the case of unconditional language modeling. Each line represents one iteration of the algorithm.\nThe green tokens are the suggestions made by the approximation model (here, a GPT-like Transformer decoder with 6M parameters\ntrained on lm1b with 8k tokens) that the target model (here, a GPT-like Transformer decoder with 97M parameters in the same setting)\naccepted, while the red and blue tokens are the rejected suggestions and their corrections, respectively. For example, in the ﬁrst line the\ntarget model was run only once, and 5 tokens were generated.\nbe accepted, while guaranteeing that the outputs from our\nsystem have the same distribution as those from the target\nmodel alone. For example, the sentence in Figure 1, con-\nsisting of 38 tokens, was generated by our method with\nonly 9 serial runs of a larger target model (97M parameters)\nthanks to a smaller and more efﬁcient approximation model\n(6M parameters), while the probability of generating it is\nunchanged.\nWe analyze our method in a variety of tasks and model\nsizes: unconditional generation from a 97M parameter GPT-\nlike model trained on lm1b, English to German translation\nand news article summarization with an 11B parameters\nT5-XXL model, and a dialog task with a 137B parameter\nLaMDA model. We implement our method and compare\nactual walltimes for T5-XXL to those of the robust T5X\nimplementation (Roberts et al., 2022), showing an out-of-\nthe-box latency improvement of 2X-3X, without any change\nto the outputs (Section 4).\nOur method is easy to employ in actual production settings,\ndoesn’t require training new models, and doesn’t change the\noutputs. Therefore, in common situations where memory\nbandwidth is the bottleneck, and compute resources are\navailable, it may be a good default to accelerate sampling\nfrom autoregressive models like Transformers.\nTo summarize, our main contributions are: (1) A generaliza-\ntion of speculative execution to the stochastic setting, with\na novel sampling method we call speculative sampling, and\n(2) A decoding mechanism we call speculative decoding that\ncan accelerate decoding from autoregressive models, with-\nout any change to the model architectures, training regimes\nand output distributions.\n2. Speculative Decoding\n2.1. Overview\nLet Mp be the target model, inference from which we’re\ntrying to accelerate, and p(xt|x<t) the distribution we get\nfrom the model for a preﬁx x<t. Let Mq be a more efﬁ-\ncient approximation model for the same task, and denote\nby q(xt|x<t) the distribution we get from the model for a\npreﬁx x<t1. The core idea is to (1) use the more efﬁcient\nmodel Mq to generate γ ∈Z+ completions (see Section 3.5\nfor how to optimally choose this parameter), then (2) use\nthe target model Mp to evaluate all of the guesses and their\nrespective probabilities from Mq in parallel, accepting all\nthose that can lead to an identical distribution, and (3) sam-\npling an additional token from an adjusted distribution to ﬁx\nthe ﬁrst one that was rejected, or to add an additional one\nif they are all accepted. That way, each parallel run of the\ntarget model Mp will produce at least one new token (so the\nnumber of serial runs of the target model can never, even\nin the worst case, be larger than the simple autoregressive\nmethod), but it can potentially generate many new tokens,\nup to γ + 1, depending on how well Mq approximates Mp.\n2.2. Standardized Sampling\nFirst, note that while there are many methods and parame-\nters of sampling, like argmax, top-k, nucleus, and setting\na temperature, and popular implementations usually treat\nthem differently at the logits level, they can all easily be cast\ninto standard sampling from an adjusted probability distribu-\ntion. For example, argmax sampling is equivalent to zeroing\nout non-max elements of the distribution and normalizing.\nWe can therefore only deal with standard sampling from a\n1We’ll use p(x) to denote p(xt|x<t) whenever the preﬁx x<t\nis clear from the context, and similarly for q(x).\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nFast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding\nprobability distribution, and cast all of the other types of\nsampling into that framework. Going forward we’ll assume\nthat p(x) and q(x) are the distributions from Mp and Mq\nrespectively, adjusted for the sampling method.\n2.3. Speculative Sampling\nTo sample x ∼p(x), we instead sample x ∼q(x), keeping\nit if q(x) ≤p(x), and in case q(x) > p(x) we reject the\nsample with probability 1−p(x)\nq(x) and sample x again from an\nadjusted distribution p′(x) = norm(max(0, p(x) −q(x)))\ninstead. It’s easy to show (see Appendix A.1) that for any\ndistributions p(x) and q(x), and x sampled in this way,\nindeed x ∼p(x).\nGiven the distribution q(x) obtained from running Mq on\na conditioning prefix, we can sample a token x1 ∼q(x).\nWe then calculate the distribution p(x) by running Mp on\nprefix while in parallel speculatively calculating the distri-\nbution of the next token x2 by running Mp on prefix+[x1].\nOnce both computations complete, we proceed as per above:\nIf x1 is rejected, we discard the computation of x2 and\nre-sample x1 from an adjusted distribution, and if x1 is ac-\ncepted, we keep both tokens. Algorithm 1 generalizes this\nidea to sample between 1 and γ + 1 tokens at once.\nAlgorithm 1 SpeculativeDecodingStep\nInputs: Mp, Mq, prefix.\n▷Sample γ guesses x1,...,γ from Mq autoregressively.\nfor i = 1 to γ do\nqi(x) ←Mq(prefix + [x1, . . . , xi−1])\nxi ∼qi(x)\nend for\n▷Run Mp in parallel.\np1(x), . . . , pγ+1(x) ←\nMp(prefix), . . . , Mp(prefix + [x1, . . . , xγ])\n▷Determine the number of accepted guesses n.\nr1 ∼U(0, 1), . . . , rγ ∼U(0, 1)\nn ←min({i −1 | 1 ≤i ≤γ, ri > pi(x)\nqi(x)} ∪{γ})\n▷Adjust the distribution from Mp if needed.\np′(x) ←pn+1(x)\nif n < γ then\np′(x) ←norm(max(0, pn+1(x) −qn+1(x)))\nend if\n▷Return one token from Mp, and n tokens from Mq.\nt ∼p′(x)\nreturn prefix + [x1, . . . , xn, t]\n3. Analysis\n3.1. Number of Generated Tokens\nLet’s analyze the reduction factor in the number of serial\ncalls to the target model, or equivalently, the expected num-\nber of tokens produced by a single run of Algorithm 1.\nDeﬁnition 3.1. The acceptance rate βx<t, given a preﬁx\nx<t, is the probability of accepting xt ∼q(xt|x<t) by\nspeculative sampling, as per Section 2.32.\nE(β) is then a natural measure of how well Mq approxi-\nmates Mp. If we make the simplifying assumption that the\nβs are i.i.d., and denote α = E(β), then the number of\ntokens produced by a single run of Algorithm 1 is a capped\ngeometric variable, with success probability 1 −α and cap\nγ + 1, and the expected number of tokens generated by\nAlgorithm 1 satisﬁes Equation (1). Calculating α\nWe’ll now derive a simple formula for calculating α given a\npreﬁx and the two models Mp and Mq. We start by deﬁning\na natural divergence DLK:\nDeﬁnition 3.2. DLK(p, q)\n=\nP\nx |p(x) −M(x)|\n=\nP\nx |q(x) −M(x)| where M(x) = p(x)+q(x)\n2\n.\nLemma 3.3. DLK(p, q) = 1 −P\nx min(p(x), q(x))\nProof. DLK(p, q) is a symmetric divergence in [0, 1].\nDLK(p, q) = 0 ⇐⇒p = q.\nDLK(p, q) = 1 ⇐⇒p and q have disjoint support.\nTheorem 3.5. β = 1 −DLK(p, q)\nProof. β\n=\nEx∼q(x)\n(\n1\nq(x) ≤p(x)\np(x)\nq(x)\nq(x) > p(x)\n=\nEx∼q(x) min(1, p(x)\nq(x)) = P\nx min(p(x), q(x))\nFinally we get:\nCorollary 3.6. α = 1 −E(DLK(p, q)) = E(min(p, q))\nSee Table 3 for empirically observed α values in our experi-\nments.\n3.3. Walltime Improvement\nWe’ve shown that with the i.i.d. assumption our algorithm\nreduces the number of calls to the target model by a factor\nof 1−αγ+1\n1−α . Note that speculative execution in general, and\nour algorithm in particular, assume that we have enough\ncompute resources to support the increased concurrency\n(Section 3.4). For the walltime anaylsis, we’ll assume that\nwe can run γ + 1 concurrent evaluations of Mp in parallel\nwithout increasing the walltime. To get the total walltime\nimprovement, we now consider the cost of running the ap-\nproximation model Mq.\nDeﬁnition 3.7. Let c, the cost coefﬁcient, be the ratio be-\ntween the time for a single run of Mq and the time for a\nsingle run of Mp.\nNote that unlike α which is an intrinsic property of the\nmodels and the task, the value of c depends on the hardware\nconﬁguration and software implementation details. In our\nexperiments where Mq is typically a couple of orders of\nmagnitude smaller than Mp, c was always less than 0.05\nand often negligibly close to 0.\nTheorem 3.8. The expected improvement factor in total\nwalltime by Algorithm 1 is\n1−αγ+1\n(1−α)(γc+1).\nProof. Denote the cost of running a single step of Mp by T.\nNow, each run of Algorithm 1 costs Tcγ + T (for running\nthe approximation model Mq γ times and running Mp once)\nand according to Equation (1) produces 1−αγ+1\n1−α\ntokens on\naverage. So the overall expected cost for producing a token\nwith Algorithm 1 is (cγ+1)(1−α)\n1−αγ+1\nT. Since the cost of pro-\nducing a single token with the standard decoding algorithm\nis T, we get the desired result.\nNote that Theorem 3.8 assumes long enough generations\n(for example, since we run Mp at least once, the improve-\nment factor is capped by the number of generated tokens).\nCorollary 3.9. If α > c, there exists γ for which we’ll get\nan improvement, and the improvement factor will be at least\n1+α\n1+c .\nProof. If we get an improvement for γ, we’d also get an\nimprovement for any 0 < γ∗< γ, so for our method to\nyield an improvement, we can evaluate Theorem 3.8 for\nγ = 1, yielding\n1−α2\n(1−α)(c+1) = 1+α\n1+c .\n3.4. Number of Arithmetic Operations\nAlgorithm 1 does γ+1 runs of Mp in parallel, so the number\nof concurrent arithmetic operations grows by a factor of\nγ+1. Now, since Algorithm 1 produces at most γ+1 tokens\nper run, the total number of arithmetic operations might be\nhigher than that of the standard decoding algorithm. When\nwe accept the sample from Mq the increased concurrency\nis “free” and the total number of operations isn’t increased3.\nWhen we reject a guess though, computation is wasted. Let’s\nnow analyze the effect of our method on the total number\nof arithmetic operations.\nDeﬁnition 3.10. Let ˆc be the ratio of arithmetic operations\nper token of the approximation model Mq to that of the\ntarget model Mp.\nTheorem 3.11. The expected factor of increase in the num-\nber of total operations of Algorithm 1 is (1−α)(γˆc+γ+1)\n1−αγ+1\n.\nProof. Denote by ˆT the number of arithmetic operations\ndone by a standard decoding baseline per token, i.e. the\nnumber of operations of a single run of Mp. Then a single\niteration of Algorithm 1 costs ˆT ˆcγ + ˆT(γ + 1) operations\n(for γ runs of Mq and γ + 1 parallel runs of Mp). Dividing\nby the expected number of tokens produced by Algorithm 1,\ni.e. Equation (1), and by ˆT, we get the desired result.\nIf α is low, the increase in the number of arithmetic oper-\nations is high, and vice-versa. Note that for Transformer\ndecoders, the total number of arithmetic operations by Al-\ngorithm 1 (not counting runs of Mq) can be bounded from\nabove by a single run of the same-size Transformer encoder.\nUnlike the total number of arithmetic operations, the total\nnumber of memory accesses can go down with our method.\nSpeciﬁcally, the target model’s weights and KV cache can\nbe read once per execution of Algorithm 1, so the number\nof memory accesses for reading them shrinks by a factor of\n1−αγ+1\n1−α , according to Equation (1).\n3.5.
